To fix (6)

To fix Power Analysis Check

What we found

We detected 1 potential power analysis.

Show details

We used the LLM model ‘vllm/DeepSeek-V41-Flash-1017-FP8’ to check the contents of 2 paragraphs that contained words suggesting they might contain power analyses.

Some essential information could not be detected: effect_size

power_type statistical_test sample_size alpha_level power effect_size effect_size_metric software
apriori correlation 39 0.05 0.8 NA other G*Power
text
An a priori power analysis was conducted using G*Power software (Faul et al., 2009). The analysis used a published effect size for the correlation between episodic detail and the flexibility score of the AUT (Addis, Pan, et al., 2016), and determined that a minimum of 39 participants was required to achieve a power of at least .80, with an alpha level of .05. This result of this power analysis was reported in the pre-registration but due to a miscommunication, it misstated that the sample size would be 70. We had aimed for a larger sample size than N = 39 given the novelty of the text analyses, but the target was N=50.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Marginal Significance

What we found

You described 1 effect with terms related to ‘marginally significant’.

Show details

You described effects with terms related to ‘marginally significant’. If p values above 0.05 are interpreted as an effect, you inflate the alpha level, and increase the Type 1 error rate. If a p value is higher than the prespecified alpha level, it should be interpreted as a non-significant result.

Text Section Header
Additionally, our sliding window approach revealed significant schema use was greatest at the beginning of the trial. Discussion

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate at which non-significant p-values are interpreted as marginally significant, see:

Olsson-Collentine A, van Assen MALM, Hartgerink CHJ (2019). “The Prevalence of Marginally Significant Results in Psychology Over Time.” Psychological Science, 30, 576–586. doi:10.1177/0956797619830326.

For the list of terms used to identifify marginally significant results, see this blog post by Matthew Hankins.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 1 imprecise p value out of 47 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
p < .01 2 Congruent > Incongruent, p < .01.

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (4)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 12 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Below, we describe some best coding practices and give the results of automatic evaluation of these practices in the code files below. This check may miss things or produce false positives if your scripts are less typical.

Code Comments

Best programming practice is to add comments to code, to explain what the code does (to yourself in the future, or peers who want to re-use your code). All your code files had comments.

Missing Files

All files loaded in the code were present in the repository.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). install.packages() calls were found in 1 code file.

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 127 columns across 8 data files.

Issues Identified

16 file/column combinations have at least one issue.

About the personal-information flags. These are pattern matches, not confirmed disclosures, and they are tuned to over-report rather than miss something. Expect false positives:

  • Column names are matched against identifying words in English and other European languages (email, telefoonnummer, geboortedatum, …). A column called phone_number flags whether or not it holds one, and an empty or already-anonymised column flags on its name alone.
  • Values are matched against the shape of an identifier. An IP address pattern also matches a version string or a dotted numeric code; a card-number pattern requires both a real issuer prefix and a checksum, but a long numeric ID can still coincide.
  • Free text is flagged when a column holds long, mostly-distinct prose — the shape open-ended answers take. Most such columns contain no names at all.

A flag means look at this column. Where the data is already de-identified (an ip column reading Anonymized, a name column the authors emptied), no action is needed.

familiarity_task.xlsx NA Not a rectangular dataset This file reads as a table but is not a usable dataset (100% of columns are free text, not variables). Store the data as a plain rectangular table (one header row, one column per variable) with a codebook.

Spreadsheet formatting such as colour, merged cells, and blank rows/columns is not preserved when data are read programmatically or exported to CSV. Store data as a plain rectangular table (one header row, one column per variable, no colour-encoded meaning) so it is machine-readable.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 8 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

A script uses something it does not create or load itself.

  • main-analyses.Rmd: condition_labels

Usually the object was still in your R session from earlier work, or another script made it.

  1. Restart R (in RStudio: Session > Restart R).
  2. Run each script from top to bottom, and fix what is missing.
  3. If a script needs results of another, save them to a file in the first script and read them in the second.
  4. Load every package with library() at the top.

Comparison with the paper

Of the 103 statistical tests reported in the paper, 7 were found in the output of the code. No script ran to the end, so most results were never produced.

All details of the Reproducibility Check

This module assesses whether the paper’s code could be run on its data, and — because execute = TRUE — actually ran it. We examined 1 R code file.

Run order

The scripts were ordered by their data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads runs first), source() calls, and numeric filename prefixes.

Order File Runs after Basis
1 main-analyses.Rmd — none

File paths

1 data path in the code does not work in the repository as deposited. We pointed it to where the file is, so the code could run: sliding-window_schema_correlations-60.csv to data/sliding-window_schema_correlations-60.csv (in main-analyses.Rmd). The code should be changed, because as written it does not find the file. 2 data paths were built at runtime with sprintf()/paste()/file.path() instead of written as a literal path — this is fragile authoring practice (it silently breaks if the code is run from a different working directory than the author’s own). We resolved all of these on a best-effort basis.

Execution

The code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 1 script: 0 ran without error, 1 errored, 0 timed out, 0 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 0 did not parse. Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions.

File Outcome Detail Time (s)
main-analyses.Rmd errored undefined_variable 41.2

Undefined-variable errors

1 script failed on an undefined variable or function (object '...' not found / could not find function "..."). This typically means the script expects a variable or function that another script defines — i.e. it is meant to be sourced into a larger session, not run on its own — or the symbol is simply never created. When another of the paper’s own script files defines the same name, this is called out below as a likely sourcing/copy-paste gap. Each is counted as an error (red).

File Missing variable Defined in
main-analyses.Rmd condition_labels (not defined anywhere in the paper’s code)

Calls that install packages in the code

1 call to install.packages(), groundhog, checkpoint, renv, pacman and the like was replaced, since they install packages themselves: groundhog.library() and p_load() by library(), the others by a statement that does nothing. The scripts ran with the packages installed before the run.

Expand a row below to see the output each script produced.

> predicted_probs3 <- function(data, idx) {
>   # Fit model
>   plaus_clm <- clmm(rate_plaus ~ FMK_CondNum + (1 | subj), data = data[idx, ], Hess = T)
>   # Compute marginal means
>   emms <- as.data.frame(emmeans(plaus_clm, ~ rate_plaus | FMK_CondNum, mode = 'prob'))
>   # Get a single vector containing probability of each rating for each level of the factor
>   out <- c()
>   for (curr_rating in 1:5) {
>     for (curr_cond in unique(data$FMK_CondNum)) {
>       sdf <- subset(emms, rate_plaus == curr_rating & FMK_CondNum == curr_cond)
>       if (nrow(sdf) > 0) {
>         prob <- sdf$prob
>       } else {
>         prob <- NA
>       }
>       out[sprintf('%s_%s', curr_cond, curr_rating)] <- prob
>     }
>   }
>   
>   return(out)
> }
> boot.res3 <- boot(data, predicted_probs3, R = 100)
> plotting_data3 <- c()
> for (curr_rating in 1:5) {
>   for (curr_cond in unique(data$FMK_CondNum)) {
>     # Identify column in boot.res corresponding to the current combination of rating and temp
>     col_name <- sprintf('%s_%s', curr_cond, curr_rating)
>     col_idx <- names(boot.res3$t0) == col_name
>     # Compute bootstrapped mean and confidence interval
>     prob <- mean(boot.res3$t[, col_idx], na.rm = T)
>     CI <- quantile(boot.res3$t[, col_idx],
>                    probs = c(0.025, 0.975), # Could be changed for a wider/narrower interval
>                    na.rm = T)
>     # Add a new row to the plotting data
>     plotting_data3 <- rbind(
>       plotting_data3,
>       data.frame(
>         FMK_CondNum = curr_cond,
>         rate_plaus = curr_rating,
>         prob = prob,
>         UCI = CI[1],
>         LCI = CI[2]
>       )
>     )
>   }
> }
> plaus_plot <- ggplot(plotting_data3, aes(y = prob, x = rate_plaus, fill = (rate_plaus))) +

Reported vs. reproduced

103 statistical tests reported in the manuscript text were checked against the extracted analysis output: 7 matched (7 fully, 0 partially) — 6.8% found.

Some reported values were only found alongside OTHER components after being separated from their own originally-reported neighbours (e.g. a mean split from its own confidence interval). The “Plausible” column marks whether that split is well-supported: TRUE when the pieces still trace back to the same underlying variable (via the output’s own row labels), FALSE when no such link was found — a FALSE match is not necessarily wrong, but is worth checking against its own Source file/Analysis columns before treating it as confirmed.

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

We recruited 53 young adults through the Rotman Research Institute Participant pool and community advertising.

Data from three participants were excluded due to distortions in audio recordings (n = 1) or task non-compliance (n = 2).

All participants gave written informed consent prior to testing and, upon completion, received $30 CAD.

During each trial, participants were presented with a detail set and the position of the location word (i.e., top, middle, bottom) was randomized across trials.

Before starting the task, participants completed one congruent and one incongruent practice trial and explained their rating choices to confirm their comprehension of the rating scales.

Participants completed all scales, but only the Analogy subscale was used for the current analyses.

For both fluency tasks, the relevant stimulus was shown onscreen and participants had one minute to list words beginning with a given letter (F, A, S) or belonging to a given category (animals, supermarket items).

To this end, we first collected descriptions of the 12 location schemas from a separate sample of participants (N = 20, aged 18-35 years, M = 27.85, SD = 4.15) recruited from Prolific.

To ensure they were members of the same collective group as the participants in the main sample, the Prolific sample only included individuals who were residents of Canada.

As in the main sample, these participants provided spoken narrative responses, albeit during a Zoom call rather than in-person.

To elicit schema descriptions, each location was presented on a PowerPoint slideshow and participants were instructed to describe each location as if they were preparing someone who had never been there: “What does it look like?

Where appropriate, mixed models with a random intercept per participant were used that account for the nested structure of the data.

As expected, participants gave higher difficulty ratings on incongruent trails compared to congruent trials (b = 0.47, z = 3.08, p = .002, OR = 1.61, 95% CI [1.18, 2.17]; see Supplemental Materials, Figure S1A).

Bonferroni-corrected pairwise contrasts revealed that, overall, participants had significantly higher perceptual (M = 0.16) compared to preparation schema use (M = 0.11; b = 0.05, t =12.91, p < .001), and significantly higher action (M = 0.15) compared to preparation schema use (b = 0.04, t = 10.79, p < .001).

Given that participants were instructed to narrate their reasoning process as well as the imagined event itself, detail ratings in this study may not have been not an ideal measure of simulation success.

In that validation study, 159 healthy adult participants (Mage = 29.83 years, SDage = 9.55, range: 18-67; 69 female) were recruited from Prolific.

All participants were fluent in English and reported a mean of 14.64 years of education (SD = 3.29).

Two participants were excluded due to task non-compliance (i.e., low effort as indicated by completing the study in < 10 mins or > 15 missing responses).

This task was a part of a one-hour online session that included other tasks, for which participants were compensated, on average, £5.24.

Ethics approval statement:

Raw audio and narratives cannot be shared publicly because these data contain potentially identifying information; ethical approval for this study permits these data to only be made available to other researchers upon reasonable request and with other measures enacted to protect participant confidentiality.

Ethics: This research was approved by the Baycrest Research Ethics Board (#19-07).

Preregistration Check

We found 1 preregistration.

id title template
6pjak Cognitive Processes Underlying Novel Future Thinking OSF Preregistration

Meta-scientific research has shown that deviations from preregistrations are often not reported or checked, and that the most common deviations concern the sample size. We recommend manually checking the full preregistration at the link above, and have provided the preregistered sample size.

id sample_size
6pjak 90 younger adults (aged 18-35 years) will be recruited to obtain at least 70 useable datasets.
Field Preregistration 1
template_name OSF Preregistration
title Cognitive Processes Underlying Novel Future Thinking
id 6pjak
link https://osf.io/6pjak
date_created 2023-11-06T00:19:47.758084
date_modified 2023-08-09T13:46:19.350123
date_registered 2023-11-06T00:19:47.741484
embargo_end_date 2025-11-10T00:00:00Z
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-6pjak-v1
research_questions We hypothesize that:

(1) (a) Schematicity and simulation success will be higher for congruent vs incongruent trials, and (b) schematicity will predict simulation success.

(2) higher executive functioning will predict (a) more successful simulation and (b) higher schematicity, (c) schematicity will mediate the effect of executive functioning on simulation success, and (d) the effects related to executive functions will be higher in the incongruent condition where demands on executive functioning are higher, whereas (e) the effect of schematicity on simulation success will be higher for congruent trials (i.e., moderation effects).
study_type Experiment - A researcher randomly assigns treatments to study subjects, this includes field or lab experiments. This is also known as an intervention experiment and includes randomized controlled trials.
blinding Personnel who analyze the data collected from the study are not aware of the treatment applied to any given group.
q5 With respect to the blinding of personnel during analysis selected above, this refers to raters who are undertaking Autobiographical Interview scoring of transcripts; they will be blind to condition, as well as participant such that each transcript from a participant will be assigned a separate blind code and scored in a random order.
study_design_overview Within subjects design with 2 factors (trial type: congruent, incongruent). For analyses of Autobiographical Interview detail scores, detail type (internal vs. external/semantic) will be included as another within-subject factor.
existing_data Registration prior to creation of data
existing_data_explanation N/A
data_collection_procedures Participants: Participants must be aged 18-35 years, fluent in English and have no history of psychiatric/neurological conditions. Participants will provide informed consent and will be paid $15/hr for their time.

Future simulation paradigm: During each trial, participants will be presented with a detail-set (location and two objects) as cues to elicit imagined future events. Details will either be congruent or incongruent; presentation trials in these conditions will be randomized. Participants will be instructed to describe a future event integrating themselves and all three details together. They will describe each event aloud, which will be audio-recorded (and later transcribed for scoring). Following each imagination trials, participants will estimate the temporal distance of the event from the present, and provide ratings (on a 5-point scale) of: amount of detail and plausibility of the imagined event; similarity to previous experiences; and task difficulty. Following completion of the task, participants rate their familiarity with each location and object.

Tasks measuring individual differences of executive functions:
- Test of Relational Reasoning (TORR; Alexander et al., 2016)
- Alternate Uses Task (AUT; Guliford, 1967)
- Verbal fluency task (Troyer, 2000).

Procedure: All tasks will be completed in one session (approximately 2 hours long). The order of the (1) simulation task and (2) individual differences tasks will be counterbalanced across participants.
sample_size 90 younger adults (aged 18-35 years) will be recruited to obtain at least 70 useable datasets.
sample_size_rationale An a priori power analysis was conducted using G*Power software (Faul et al., 2007), based on a published effect size for the correlation between episodic detail and the Categories score of the AUT (Madore et al., 2015). This analysis determined we will need a minimum of 39 participants to achieve a power of at least .80, assuming an alpha error probability of .05.
manipulated_variables Simulation Condition: We will manipulate the congruence of the location and object details used as cues, modulating the usefulness of location frameworks for scaffolding the integration of details into coherent events. To validate this manipulation, we had a previous online sample of participants (N = 25) rate how easy it was to create an event that integrated the objects and location and confirmed that ease of integration was higher for congruent versus incongruent trials. Importantly, semantic similarity of the words comprising the congruent vs. congruent stimuli did not differ to avoid differences in semantic similarity driving the ease of integration.
measured_variables Simulation success measures:
(a) Participants’ event narratives will be scored using the adapted Autobiographical Interview (AI) scoring protocol (Addis, Wong, & Schacter, 2008; Levine et al., 2002) to derive the number of internal, external and semantic details in each event narrative. While internal and external detail scores are our primary measures, we will also re-run analyses using semantic scores (cf. Devitt et al., 2017).
(b) Participants’ subjective ratings of event detail and plausibility
Other measures:
(c) Participants’ ratings of event novelty and simulation task difficulty will be used as manipulation checks.
(d) Participants estimated temporal distance of the simulated events, and word counts of event narratives will be used to rule out possible confounds.

Measure of schematicity:
Using the Universal Sentence Encoder (USE; Cer et al., 2019), event narratives from the simulation task will be turned into a 512-dimension vectors. The vectors for participants’ event narratives will then be compared to vectors of schema templates (derived from descriptions of the location stimuli generated by an independent sample) using correlation measures (e.g. cosine similarity). Thus, the measure of schematicity for each trial is the correlation coefficient between event narrative vector and schema template vector.

Individual difference measures of executive function:
- The TORR has 4 subtests resulting in 4 subscores (analogical, anomalous, antinomous and antithetical reasoning) and a total score. Although our primary analyses will focus on the analogy subscore (see Indices), if there is insufficient variance in this subtest, we will use the TORR total score.
- Responses on the AUT will be scored using a standard scoring protocol (Madore et al., 2015), resulting in 5 scores (total uses, total appropriate uses, categories, categories of appropriate uses, elaboration and creativity). Although our primary analyses will focus on categories as a measure of flexibility (see Indices), if there is insufficient variance, we will use the total uses score.
- Verbal fluency has 2 subtests: phonemic and semantic clustering, which will be scored using the scoring rules to derive mean cluster size for each (Troyer et al., 2000). Other scores from this measure are: raw number of switches, total number of correct words for semantic and phonemic verbal fluency (Troyer et al., 1997); we will also measure the total number of clusters for semantic and phonemic fluency. Although our primary analyses will focus on mean cluster size, our exploratory analyses will include raw number of switches, total correct words and total number of clusters (see Exploratory Analysis).
indices Analogical Reasoning Index:
We predict a high correlation between the TORR analogy score and AUT categories score. If so, we will collapse them together to create an “analogical reasoning index” to be used in the analyses below. To do this, we will z-transform these scores before averaging them. In the event that these two scores are not correlated, we will use them separately in the analyses.
statistical_tests Rule out confounds
- Two-tailed paired t-test, IV: Condition, DV: Temporal Distance Rating
- Two-tailed paired t-test, IV: Condition, DV: Word count of event narratives

Manipulation checks
- One-tailed paired t-test, IV: Condition, DV: Difficulty Rating
- One-tailed paired samples t-test, IV: Condition, DV: Novelty Rating

Hypothesis 1(a) Simulation success by condition
- One-tailed paired samples t-test, IV: Condition, DV: Detail rating
- One-tailed paired samples t-test, IV: Condition, DV: Plausibility rating
- 2 (Condition: Incongruent, Congruent) x 2 (AI Detail type: Internal, External) Repeated Measures ANOVA, DV: Number of details

Hypothesis 1(b) Schematicity by condition
- One-tailed paired samples t-test, IV: Condition, DV: Avg similarity

Hypothesis 2:
- Mediation Analysis: executive functioning (X), simulation success (Y), schematicity (M), Condition (C)
- Hypothesis 2 (a) X → Y
- Hypothesis 2 (b) X → M
- Hypothesis 2 (c) X + M → Y
- Hypothesis 2 (d) X * C → Y; X * C → M; X * C + M → Y
- Hypothesis 2 (e) M * C → Y

Note that because we have multiple DVs measuring executive functioning and simulation success as listed below, we will use dimensionality reduction techniques as appropriate.

Executive functioning DVs:
- Analogical reasoning index
- Phonemic fluency mean cluster score
- Semantic fluency mean cluster score.
Simulation Success DVs:
- AI internal detail
- AI external (or semantic) detail
- Subjective detail rating
- Subjective plausibility rating
transformations Prior to statistical analyses, we will conduct Shapiro-Wilk tests to ensure normal distribution within subjective ratings. In the event of a significant deviation, we will run a logarithmic or exponential transformation until normal.

We predict a high correlation between the TORR analogy score and AUT flexibility score. Consequently, we will collapse them together to create an “analogical reasoning metric” to be used in the analyses above. To do this, we will z-transform participant scores on these two measures. Equally weighted, the z-scores will be averaged to create an analogical reasoning metric.
inference_criteria We will use the standard p < .05 threshold for determining rejection of the null hypothesis. We will adjust p-values for multiple comparisons using a Bonferroni correction as appropriate.
data_exclusion_criteria Outliers will be excluded on a trial basis, if the event description is non-compliant to task instructions (i.e., a meaningless response is provided); if a future event is estimated as being more than 5 years into the future; and if the imagined event is as identical to a past experience (“5” on the similarity rating scale).
missing_data_handling For simulation data, available trials will be used for analysis. If an individual difference measure is missing, the participant will excluded from all analyses, and (where possible) a replacement participant will be tested.
exploratory_analyses As noted above, we may re-run analyses using the Semantic detail score from the Autobiographical Interview in place of the External detail score. We may also include the raw switching score, total number of clusters and total number of correct words from semantic and verbal fluency (or a composite score) as additional dependent variables in our mediation analysis. We will likely also examine whether schematicity is higher earlier in the simulation trials.
additional_comments References:
