Effects of Schema Use Measures on Simulation Success 11

Whether the use of the full schema and sub-schemas influenced simulation success was tested 12 using CLMMs for detail and plausibility ratings and an LMM for internal word count. Contrary to 13 our hypotheses, none of the schema use metrics predicted detail ratings (all p values > .559) or 14 internal word counts (p values > .161; for full statistics, see Table S4-6 in Supplemental Materials).

15

However, action schema use was positively associated with plausibility (b = 11.24, z = 2.76, p = 16 .006, OR = 7.64×10⁴, 95% CI [26.24, 2.23×10⁸). This effect was moderated by condition (b = -17 4.93, z = -2.11, p = .035, OR = 0.01, 95% CI [0.0001, 0.7101]). Follow-up simple slope analyses indicated that greater action schema use was associated with higher plausibility ratings in the congruent (b = 6.32, z = 3.26, p = .001, OR = 553.27, 95% CI [5.36, 5.72×10⁴]) but not the incongruent condition (b = 1.39, z = 1.01, p = .313, OR = 138.14, 95% CI [0.51, 3.74×10⁴]).

Effects of Executive Measures on Schema Use and Simulation Success

Before examining the contribution of executive functions to schema use and simulation success, we determined whether the measures from the TORR, AUT and fluency tasks could be collapsed into a single latent variable (as per our pre-registration plan). Although there was a positive correlation between semantic and phonemic clustering scores (r = .35, p = .013), this did not survive a Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons (α = .008), and no other correlations between executive measures were significant (p values > .118; see Table S2 in Supplemental Materials). Thus, we computed separate analyses using each executive measure as a predictor.

LMM analyses revealed no significant effects of any of the executive measures on the use of the full schema or sub-schemas use (all p values > .483; see Table S3 in Supplemental Materials), nor were there any significant interaction effects between executive measures and condition (all p values > .439). The effect of each executive measure on simulation success was estimated using CLMMs for detail and plausibility ratings and an LMM for internal word count (for full statistical results, see Tables S7-9 Supplemental Materials). We found that greater analogical reasoning abilities were associated with higher detail ratings (b = 0.51, z = 2.75, p = .006, OR = 1.66, 95% CI [1.16, 2.38]). Interestingly, this effect was moderated by condition (b = -0.22, z = -2.41, p = .016, OR = 0.80, 95% CI [0.67, 0.96]). Follow-up simple slope analyses indicated that greater analogical reasoning abilities were associated with higher detail ratings in the congruent condition (b = 0.29, z = 2.17 p = .030, OR = 1.34, 95% CI [1.03, 1.73]) but not in the incongruent condition (b = 0.07, z = 0.54, p = .592, OR = 1.07, 95% CI [0.83, 1.40]). However, no other executive measure was significantly associated with detail ratings (p values > .488), and none of the executive measures had effects on plausibility ratings (p values > .116) or internal word count (p values >.572).

Discussion

The current study examined the role of schemas and executive functioning in the simulation of novel future events. Our novel RSA approach provides some of the first evidence that schemas are used in service of imagining novel future events, particularly during the early phases of the trial. Further, we found that schema use varied by condition: full schema and preparation schema use was higher for congruent trials, while action schema use was higher for incongruent trials.

Additionally, action schema use predicted greater event plausibility, particularly in the congruent condition. Notably, executive capacity was not related to schema use, although relational reasoning ability was associated with higher subjective detail on congruent trials.

As expected, simulation success was higher for congruent than incongruent trials. Specifically, plausibility ratings were higher for congruent vs. incongruent trials, indicating that providing a useful location schema supported generation of realistic events. Inconsistent with our hypotheses, there was no difference in detail ratings between conditions. However, as would be expected, an objective measure of internal (episodic) detail was higher for narratives from the congruent versus incongruent trials. Taken together with evidence that location cues positively influence the generation of non-spatial details (Hallford et al., 2023; Robin & Moscovitch, 2014), in part because they organize knowledge about the objects and actions likely to occur within those locations (Tversky & Hemenway, 1983), these findings further show that spatial schemas can scaffold episodic simulation (Maguire & Mullally, 2013).

Although these findings suggest that the provision of schemas can influence the simulation of future events, it does not provide direct evidence that schematic knowledge is incorporated into imagined events. Utilizing a narrative form of RSA, we demonstrated that pre-existing knowledge structures -in this case, location schemas -are incorporated into future event narratives.

Specifically, the imagined events comprised semantic information that mapped onto location descriptions provided by a separate sample of participants, with higher cosine similarities between event narratives and the schema template for the corresponding (vs. non-cued) location.

Importantly, these findings were evident for the full schema and all three sub-schemas (action, perceptual and preparation), while also controlling for similarity to schema templates for all other non-cued locations. Additionally, our sliding window approach revealed significant schema use was greatest at the beginning of the trial. Thus, event construction appears to involve a shift from initial contextualization and "scene setting" using schematic information to subsequent elaboration of idiosyncratic event-specific details (D' Argembeau & Mathy, 2011; Zeithamova et al., 2012).

While the use of full schemas was significantly higher in the congruent than the incongruent condition, this pattern was not evident for all sub-schemas. For instance, there was no difference in perceptual schema use between conditions -and across both conditions perceptual schema use was higher than preparation and action schema. This finding suggests that perceptual location knowledge is fundamental to constructing an imagined event, regardless of congruency (Maguire & Mullally, 2013). Surprisingly, action schema use was higher in the incongruent condition, likely because participants often integrated incongruent details into events by involving them in locationspecific actions. One influential theory of schemas conceives of them as "scripts" that prescribe a set of actions containing roles or "slots" that can be filled (Schank & Ableson, 1977). Although the actions are more fixed, the specific objects or people that fill these slots is flexible. For example, given location Spa and objects Pebble and Beehive, participants often employed a "spa action" schema to integrate the objects into the event (e.g., hot-stone massages and honey facials) rather than introducing a new action (e.g., beehive fumigation) from outside the "spa" script.

Although action schema use was higher on incongruent trials, its association with simulation success was stronger for congruent versus incongruent trials with respect to plausibility. This finding suggests that even though participants referred to action schemas more during incongruent trials, they appeared to use this schematic information more effectively to scaffold the generation of plausible event content -consistent with the view that schemas establish expectations for how events should unfold (Bartlett, 1932). Moreover, we also use schematic knowledge to evaluate the likelihood of future events (Tversky & Khaneman, 2015), and plausibility judgments could have been influenced by how well the narrative aligned with relevant schema (Canter et al., 2003) which was more likely to be the case when the location and objects were congruent.

Contrary to our hypotheses, none of the other schema use metrics predicted plausibility, nor were any schema use metrics predictive of the other measures of simulation success (i.e., detail ratings and internal content). Given that participants were instructed to narrate their reasoning process as well as the imagined event itself, detail ratings in this study may not have been not an ideal measure of simulation success. Notably, however, the number of internal details were also not associated with schema use. It is possible that other characteristics of simulations, such as coherence, might have been more sensitive indicators of the ability to successfully integrate the location and object details into an imagined future event.

Another explanation for this finding may also be applicable to the few associations between executive functions -analogical reasoning, divergent thinking and strategic retrieval -and both schema use and simulation success: our simulation task may not have imposed significant executive demands. Consistent with this suggestion, difficulty ratings were only moderate across both conditions (2.67-2.92 on a 5-point scale). This congruency manipulation did not use objects that strongly violated location-based expectations (cf. Audrain & McAndrews, 2022) or the random combinations of personally-relevant people, places and objects (Roberts et al., 2017; van Mulukom et al., 2016), and as such, the object-location combinations may have only been somewhat unexpected and thus relatively easy to incorporate. Moreover, there is a distinction between the flexible use of a location schema (which was captured by our RSA measure) and the creation of a novel schematic framework for integrating details (which was not). It may be the creation of novel schemas that creates executive demand, whereas the use of a readily available schema may involve direct, automatic retrieval (Conway & Pleydell-Pearce, 2000). Thus, perhaps we found no relationship between executive functioning and schema use because our measure was sensitive only to our pre-determined location schemas.

It should be noted we did find that individuals with stronger analogical reasoning abilities generated (subjectively) more detailed future simulations. In general, such individuals are better able to integrate information, draw inferences, and generate detailed, plausible future events (Alexander, Jablansky, et al., 2016; Roberts et al., 2018). Additionally, they likely engaged in more thorough reasoning which may have led to greater perceived detail. In contrast, detail ratings were only weakly correlated with internal word count (R² = 0.06), suggesting the relationship between relational reasoning and detail may be driven by participants' verbal reasoning rather than exclusively by the amount of episodic content generated.

In conclusion, the present study examined the role of schemas and executive functioning in future simulation, introducing a novel RSA method to quantify schema use. Given the adaptive functions of future simulation, understanding factors that support one to imagine their future has important implications. This knowledge may support the development of interventions aimed at addressing deficits in future simulation and promoting the use of appropriate schemas and relational reasoning particularly in older adults (Cole et al., 2013) or individuals with depression, who may rely on maladaptive schemas when simulating the future (Addis et al., 2025; Addis, Hach, et al., 2016).

Study Disclosures

Pre-registration: The hypotheses, methods and analysis plan were preregistered on the Open Science Framework (https://osf.io/6pjak) prior to data collection. Non-preregistered analyses reported below are identified as exploratory, and deviations from the analysis plan are noted where relevant and listed in full in the Supplemental Materials. Materials: All study materials are provided in Supplemental Materials or publicly available on OSF (https://osf.io/f9dey/). Data: Deidentified quantitative data are publicly available on OSF (https://osf.io/f9dey/). Raw audio and narratives cannot be shared publicly because these data contain potentially identifying information; ethical approval for this study permits these data to only be made available to other researchers upon reasonable request and with other measures enacted to protect participant confidentiality.

Analysis scripts: R scripts are publicly available on OSF (https://osf.io/f9dey/).

Supplemental Materials

Building the future: Schemas contribute to episodic simulation

Deviations from the pre-registration

Due to a miscommunication, the pre-registration plan (https://osf.io/6pjak) misstated the target sample size as N=70. As reported in the pre-registration, the power analysis indicated a sample size of N=39. We had aimed for a larger sample size than this, given the novelty of the text analyses, but the target was N=50.

The pre-registration plan specified that we would use paired t-tests and repeated measures ANOVAs to test our hypotheses. However, we opted to run mixed models that enabled us to include a random intercept per participant. Also, for ratings data we used cumulative link mixed models which are the statistically appropriate test for ordinal data (Liddell & Kruschke, 2018). Both types of analyses yielded the same pattern of results.

We planned to use the cosine similarity between a narrative and the schema template for the cued location as the measure of schema use. However, narratives exhibited a significant degree of similarity to all schema templates regardless of location. Thus, we controlled for this by subtracting from the similarity to the cued location the similarity to schema templates for all other non-cued locations.

We did not compute an analogical reasoning metric as we found that the Test of Relational Reasoning (TORR) analogy score and Alternate Uses Task (AUT) flexibility scores were not correlated (as reported in the paper).

We had also planned to run moderated-mediation analyses to explore the direct and indirect effects of each executive functioning measure, as well as schema use, on our three simulation success variables. However, as many of these variables were not significantly correlated with one another (e.g., see Table S2 below), we did not pursue these analyses.

Reference:

Liddell, T., & Kruschke, J. K. (2018). Analyzing ordinal data with metric models: What could possibly go wrong? Journal of Experimental Social Psychology, 79, 328-348.

Validation of the congruence manipulation

The 6 congruent and 6 incongruent detail sets (location and 2 objects) used in the current study were taken from a larger set of 160 detail sets that had been validated in a previous online study.

In that validation study, 159 healthy adult participants (Mage = 29.83 years, SDage = 9.55, range: 18-67; 69 female) were recruited from Prolific. All participants were fluent in English and reported a mean of 14.64 years of education (SD = 3.29). Two participants were excluded due to task non-compliance (i.e., low effort as indicated by completing the study in < 10 mins or > 15 missing responses).

On each trial, participants imagined a future event involving the three details, and rated how easily the location could be integrated with the two objects into a coherent event. Ease of integration was rated on a 7-point Likert scale (1 = extremely difficult to7 = extremely easy). Each participant rated a total subset of 20 congruent and 20 incongruent sets; each detail set was rated by between 17 and 21 participants. This task was a part of a one-hour online session that included other tasks, for which participants were compensated, on average, £5.24.
